It’s when that door closes that it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you start feeling anxious before you even enter one of those slow elevators or a small locked room?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes I do, if I can see what I’m walking into. Like if I’m at the museum and there’s one of those elevators, or a little storage-type room with no window, I’ll start thinking about it before I even step in.\n\nI know it’s not the same as being locked up somewhere, you know, I know that in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not always, no. If I don’t notice it much ahead of time, it might not start until I’m right there. But if I see it’s one of those little storage rooms, or that elevator at the museum with no window, then I start getting that feeling before I go in.

I know it’s not really the same as being locked up somewhere, but my mind kind of treats it like trouble once I see there’s no window and that door’s going to shut tight. I don’t know why those museum elevators always seem so slow—maybe that’s just me paying too much attention to it.

I mean, if it’s one of those slow elevators or a little room where the door’s locked, it hits me right away—my chest jumps and I just want to get back out before I can even think straight.\n\nFunny thing is, I’ll be fine walking around the building, talking to folks, you know, and then that door closes and—well, there it is. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pretty much, yes—when I know I’m about to get into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door shuts, I start bracing myself. But the real rush hits when I actually step in. My chest jumps and I just get that “get out” feeling before I can even think straight.

Funny thing, I used to not think twice about elevators years ago… old department stores, the ones with the operator, you know? But now, if it’s slow or tight, I’m already uneasy before I go in.

Not really other situations, no. It’s that same kind of place—like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but my mind treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight.

I don’t really think so… not out of the blue like that. If I’m just sitting in the kitchen or out walking, I don’t usually get that sudden rush.

I don’t know why those museum elevators always seem so slow—maybe that’s just me paying too much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of a sudden jump in your chest and feeling like you need to get out when you're not thinking about or entering a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: No, not when I’m just sitting around or thinking about something else. It’s mostly when I’m actually stepping into one of those slow elevators or a little locked room. That’s when my chest kind of jumps right away and I get that feeling like I need to get out before I can even think straight.\n\nIf I’m just walking around outside or sitting in a regular room, I’m usually alright. But no, it’s mostly that closed-in, no-window, door-shut kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes, in situations other than being in elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. That “peaks real fast” feeling you’re talking about, that’s mostly when I’m in one of those tight spots.\n\nOther times, I might get annoyed or worked up—like if I can’t find my glasses, which seems to happen every other day now—but it’s not that same sudden fear. At least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… not out of the blue like that. If I’m just sitting in the kitchen or out walking, I don’t usually get that sudden rush.\n\nThough, you know, sometimes I’ll get a little unsettled if the TV’s too loud or if I’m trying to remember where I put something—last week I put the mail in the bread box, don’t ask me why—but that’s not the same thing you mean, I don’t think. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, in those situations, yes. When I step into a slow elevator, or one of those small rooms where the door locks, it hits me right away. My chest kind of jumps, and all I can think is, “I need to get back out.”

It’s funny, I used to take elevators without thinking much—well, maybe not funny, but you know what I mean. Now if it’s slow, I start noticing every little sound, like the cables or the door, and before I can even get my thoughts in order, I’m already wanting out. Same with a little locked room. It comes on quick.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, mostly that—closed-in places. Like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up somewhere, but my head doesn’t seem to care. Once I notice there’s no window and the door shuts tight, it just feels like trouble.

I don’t mean every small place, necessarily… like my pantry at home, well, that’s different, though I try not to stand in there too long either. But elevators, little locked rooms—those are the ones that set it off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… it’s kind of both, I think. Not just the walls being close, but what my mind says about it once I’m in there. Like I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but the minute I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble.

I remember one museum elevator, it had those polished doors—my wife liked that museum, lots of old clocks, though I never understood why they all had to tick so loud—and I wasn’t trapped or anything. But once the door closed, that was it. My head went, “this isn’t right, you need out.” So it’s not only the feeling, it’s what I imagine could happen in there, I guess.

I mean, if it’s one of those slow elevators or a little room where the door’s locked, it hits me right away—my chest jumps and I just want to get back out before I can even think straight.\n\nFunny thing is, I’ll be fine walking around the building, talking to folks, you know, and then that door closes and—well, there it is. So yes, it’s not something I have to wait on. It comes on quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have this feeling of needing to get out or these physical symptoms when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: No, not really—not that same way. Funny, I used to take elevators without thinking much, back when I’d go visit offices downtown, but now when that door closes, I just feel like I’ve got to get back out before I can even gather myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of sudden chest jumps and feeling like you need to get out when you're not in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can be doing regular things, you know, minding my business, and it’s not there. It’s when that door closes that it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you start feeling anxious before you even enter one of those slow elevators or a small locked room?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes I do, if I can see what I’m walking into. Like if I’m at the museum and there’s one of those elevators, or a little storage-type room with no window, I’ll start thinking about it before I even step in.\n\nI know it’s not the same as being locked up somewhere, you know, I know that in my head. But the minute I see there’s no window and that door’s going to shut tight, my mind treats it like trouble. I don’t know why those museum elevators always seem so slow—maybe that’s just me paying too much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of a sudden jump in your chest and feeling like you need to get out when you're not thinking about or entering a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: No, not when I’m just sitting around or thinking about something else. It’s really when I step into one of those slow elevators or a small locked room. Then my chest jumps right away and I get this strong feeling like I’ve got to get back out before I can even think straight.\n\nI mean, I can be fine beforehand if I’m not paying attention to it—like thinking about groceries or where I parked the car, you know how parking lots are these days—but once I’m in that closed-in spot, that’s when it hits.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel like you're having a heart attack or are going to pass out when you're not in a closed-in space?\n\nPatient: Not really like that out of nowhere, no. It’s more when I step into one of those slow elevators or a small locked room — then my chest kind of jumps right away and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight.\n\nIf I’m just out in the open, or sitting at home, I don’t usually get that. Though, you know, at my age you do think about your heart sometimes — my neighbor was always talking about his — but for me this feeling is tied to being in that closed-in spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. It’s mostly when I’m actually stepping into one of those slow elevators or a little locked room. That’s when my chest kind of jumps right away and I get that feeling like I need to get out before I can even think straight.\n\nIf I’m just walking around outside or sitting in a regular room, I’m usually alright. Though sometimes those old buildings, you know, they have hallways that feel tight too—but really it’s the elevator or the small room with the door shut that does it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of that. I mean, I get ordinary worries like anybody—bills, the weather, whether the car’s gonna start—but the thing that really gets me is those closed-in places.\n\nLike I was saying, whenever I step into a slow elevator or a small locked room, my chest jumps right away, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight. That’s the main situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel anxious before entering a closed-in space, like a slow elevator or a small locked room?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes—when I know I’m about to get into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door shuts, I start bracing myself. But the real rush hits when I actually step in. My chest jumps and I just get that “get out” feeling before I can even think straight.\n\nFunny thing, I used to not think twice about elevators years ago… old department stores, the ones with the operator, you know? I mean, I get ordinary worries like anybody—bills, the weather, whether the car’s gonna start—but the thing that really gets me is those closed-in places.\n\nLike I was saying, whenever I step into a slow elevator or a small locked room, my chest jumps right away, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight. That’s the main situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel anxious before entering a closed-in space, like a slow elevator or a small locked room?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes—when I know I’m about to get into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door shuts, I start bracing myself. But the real rush hits when I actually step in. My chest jumps and I just get that “get out” feeling before I can even think straight.\n\nFunny thing, I used to not think twice about elevators years ago… old department stores, the ones with the operator, you know? But now, if it’s slow or tight, I’m already uneasy before I go in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in any situations other than being in elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really other situations, no. It’s that same kind of place—like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but my mind treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight.\n\nFunny, at the museum I used to like looking at the old train displays, and then you get near one of those back hallways or little rooms, and I’m already thinking about the door more than what I came to see. But no, it’s mostly that closed-in, no-window, door-shut kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes, in situations other than being in elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. That “peaks real fast” feeling you’re talking about, that’s mostly when I’m in one of those tight spots.\n\nOther times, I might get annoyed or worked up—like if I can’t find my glasses, which seems to happen every other day now—but it’s not that same sudden fear. At least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… not out of the blue like that. If I’m just sitting in the kitchen or out walking, I don’t usually get that sudden rush.\n\nThough, you know, sometimes I’ll get a little unsettled if the TV’s too loud or if I’m trying to remember where I put something—last week I put the mail in the bread box, don’t ask me why—but that’s not the same thing you mean, I don’t think. The quick, sharp fear… I only really notice that with those closed-in places.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, in those situations, yes. When I step into a slow elevator, or one of those small rooms where the door locks, it hits me right away. My chest kind of jumps, and all I can think is, “I need to get back out.”\n\nIt’s funny, I used to take elevators without thinking much—well, maybe not funny, but you know what I mean. Now if it’s slow, I start noticing every little sound, like the cables or the door, and before I can even get my thoughts in order, I’m already wanting out. Same with a little locked room. It comes on quick.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that—closed-in places. Like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up somewhere, but my head doesn’t seem to care. Once I notice there’s no window and the door shuts tight, it just feels like trouble.\n\nI don’t mean every small place, necessarily… like my pantry at home, well, that’s different, though I try not to stand in there too long either. But elevators, little locked rooms—those are the ones that set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of the physical feeling of being in closed-in spaces, or is it also about what might happen to you in those spaces?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s kind of both, I think. Not just the walls being close, but what my mind says about it once I’m in there. Like I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but the minute I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble.\n\nI remember one museum elevator, it had those polished doors—my wife liked that museum, lots of old clocks, though I never understood why they all had to tick so loud—and I wasn’t trapped or anything. But once the door closed, that was it. My head went, “this isn’t right, you need out.” So it’s not only the feeling, it’s what I imagine could happen in there, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more when I step into one of those slow elevators or a small locked room — then my chest kind of jumps right away and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight.\n\nIf I’m just out in the open, or sitting at home, I don’t usually get that. Though, you know, at my age you do think about your heart sometimes — my neighbor was always talking about his — but for me this feeling is tied to being in that closed-in spot.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. It’s mostly when I’m actually stepping into one of those slow elevators or a little locked room. That’s when my chest kind of jumps right away and I get that feeling like I need to get out before I can even think straight.\n\nIf I’m just walking around outside or sitting in a regular room, I’m usually alright. Though sometimes those old buildings, you know, they have hallways that feel tight too—but really it’s the elevator or the small room with the door shut that does it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of that. I mean, I get ordinary worries like anybody—bills, the weather, whether the car’s gonna start—but the thing that really gets me is those closed-in places.\n\nLike I was saying, whenever I step into a slow elevator or a small locked room, my chest jumps right away, and I get this strong need to get back out before I can even think straight. That’s the main situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you always feel anxious before entering a closed-in space, like a slow elevator or a small locked room?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes—when I know I’m about to get into one of those slow elevators, or a small room where the door shuts, I start bracing myself. But the real rush hits when I actually step in. My chest jumps and I just get that “get out” feeling before I can even think straight.\n\nFunny thing, I used to not think twice about elevators years ago… old department stores, the ones with the operator, you know? But now, if it’s slow or tight, I’m already uneasy before I go in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in any situations other than being in elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Not really other situations, no. It’s that same kind of place—like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but my mind treats it like trouble as soon as I see there’s no window and that door shuts tight.\n\nFunny, at the museum I used to like looking at the old train displays, and then you get near one of those back hallways or little rooms, and I’m already thinking about the door more than what I came to see. But no, it’s mostly that closed-in, no-window, door-shut kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes, in situations other than being in elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not that I’ve noticed. That “peaks real fast” feeling you’re talking about, that’s mostly when I’m in one of those tight spots.\n\nOther times, I might get annoyed or worked up—like if I can’t find my glasses, which seems to happen every other day now—but it’s not that same sudden fear. At least I don’t think it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes when you're not in a closed-in space like an elevator or a locked room?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… not out of the blue like that. If I’m just sitting in the kitchen or out walking, I don’t usually get that sudden rush.\n\nThough, you know, sometimes I’ll get a little unsettled if the TV’s too loud or if I’m trying to remember where I put something—last week I put the mail in the bread box, don’t ask me why—but that’s not the same thing you mean, I don’t think. The quick, sharp fear… I only really notice that with those closed-in places.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes in any situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, in those situations, yes. When I step into a slow elevator, or one of those small rooms where the door locks, it hits me right away. My chest kind of jumps, and all I can think is, “I need to get back out.”\n\nIt’s funny, I used to take elevators without thinking much—well, maybe not funny, but you know what I mean. Now if it’s slow, I start noticing every little sound, like the cables or the door, and before I can even get my thoughts in order, I’m already wanting out. Same with a little locked room. It comes on quick.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that—closed-in places. Like, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t really the same as being locked up somewhere, but my head doesn’t seem to care. Once I notice there’s no window and the door shuts tight, it just feels like trouble.\n\nI don’t mean every small place, necessarily… like my pantry at home, well, that’s different, though I try not to stand in there too long either. But elevators, little locked rooms—those are the ones that set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of the physical feeling of being in closed-in spaces, or is it also about what might happen to you in those spaces?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s kind of both, I think. Not just the walls being close, but what my mind says about it once I’m in there. Like I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere, but the minute I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight, my mind treats it like trouble.\n\nI remember one museum elevator, it had those polished doors—my wife liked that museum, lots of old clocks, though I never understood why they all had to tick so loud—and I wasn’t trapped or anything. But once the door closed, that was it. My head went, “this isn’t right, you need out.” So it’s not only the feeling, it’s what I imagine could happen in there, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms, without any other concerns?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s really those closed-in places. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere—my wife always liked museums, you know, especially the ones with all the old displays—but my mind doesn’t seem to make that distinction.\n\nThe minute I notice there’s no window and the door shuts tight, it just feels like trouble. Like I shouldn’t be in there.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in closed-in spaces like elevators or locked rooms, or is there something else that also scares you in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the closed-in part, yes, but not just the size of the place. It’s when I see there’s no window and the door shuts tight—then my mind starts treating it like trouble.\n\nLike, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn’t the same as being locked up somewhere. I know that. My wife used to like those museum trips, and I’d be standing there trying to act normal, looking at the signs and all that, but if we had to take an elevator… once those doors closed, that was different. It’s like my head says, “You shouldn’t be in here.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
